For standard Mirror-Prox, the Bregman-Fejér inequality is:

E[D_{\phi}(z,G_{n+1})\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}]\leq D_{\phi}(z,G_{n})-\eta_{n}E[\langle g_{n}(\tilde{G}_{n+1}),G_{n}-z\rangle\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}]+\varepsilon_{n}(\cdot,z)\quad\text{a.s.}

Since
E[g_{n}(\tilde{G}_{n+1})\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}]=\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1})
, we have
E[\langle g_{n}(\tilde{G}_{n+1}),G_{n}-z\rangle\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}]=\langle\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1}),G_{n}-z\rangle
. For
z\in Z
,
\nabla f(z)=0
, and by monotonicity of
f
:

\langle\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1}),G_{n}-z\rangle\geq\langle\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1})-\nabla f(z),G_{n}-z\rangle\geq 0.

Since
\sum_{n}\varepsilon_{n}(\cdot,z)<\infty
a.s.,
3.1
(part v) implies:

\sum_{n}\eta_{n}\langle\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1}),G_{n}-z\rangle<\infty\quad\text{a.s.}

By Hölder’s inequality:

\langle\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1}),G_{n}-z\rangle\leq\|\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1})\|_{*}\|G_{n}-z\|_{X}.

Since
(G_{n})
is Bregman-bounded a.s. (
3.1
, part v.a),
\|G_{n}-z\|_{X}
is bounded a.s., and
E\|g_{n}(G_{n})\|_{*}^{2}\to 0
a.s. implies
g_{n}(G_{n})\to 0
a.s. (noting
E\|g_{n}(\tilde{G}_{n+1})\|_{*}^{2}\approx E\|g_{n}(G_{n})\|_{*}^{2}
). By the dominated convergence theorem
[
Dinculeanu 2000
, Theorem 2.3.5]
,
g_{n}(G_{n})\to 0
strongly a.s. in
X^{*}
.

For over-relaxed Mirror-Prox, the inequality is:

E[D_{\phi}(z,G_{n+1})\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}]\leq D_{\phi}(z,G_{n})-E[\lambda_{n}(2-\lambda_{n})\Theta_{n}\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}]+Y_{n}(\cdot,z)\quad\text{a.s.},

where
\Theta_{n}=\eta_{n}^{2}\|g_{n}(\tilde{G}_{n+1})\|_{*}^{2}
(Type B) or
(\lambda_{n}\eta_{n})^{2}\|g_{n}(\tilde{G}_{n+1})\|_{*}^{2}
(Type A), and
Y_{n}(\cdot,z)=\lambda_{n}\varepsilon_{n}(\cdot,z)
. Since
\sum_{n}Y_{n}(\cdot,z)=\sum_{n}\lambda_{n}\varepsilon_{n}(\cdot,z)<\infty
a.s. (as
\lambda_{n}\in L^{\infty}
), and
\inf_{n}E[\lambda_{n}(2-\lambda_{n})]>0
,
3.1
gives:

E[\lambda_{n}(2-\lambda_{n})\Theta_{n}\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}]=E[\lambda_{n}(2-\lambda_{n})]E[\Theta_{n}\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}].

Thus,
3.3
(part i.a) implies:

\sum_{n}E[\lambda_{n}(2-\lambda_{n})\Theta_{n}]=\sum_{n}E[\lambda_{n}(2-\lambda_{n})]E[\eta_{n}^{2}\|g_{n}(\tilde{G}_{n+1})\|_{*}^{2}]<\infty\quad\text{a.s.}

Since
\inf_{n}E[\lambda_{n}(2-\lambda_{n})]\geq\epsilon>0
, and
\sum_{n}\eta_{n}^{2}E\|g_{n}(G_{n})\|_{*}^{2}<\infty
a.s.,
g_{n}(G_{n})\to 0
strongly a.s. in
X^{*}
.

(b) Weak convergence of
G_{n}
:

For all variants, since
\sum_{n}Y_{n}(\cdot,z)<\infty
a.s. and
\mathfrak{W}(G_{n})\subset Z
a.s. (
A.1
),
3.1
(part v.d) and
3.3
(part i.c) imply
G_{n}\rightharpoonup G^{\star}\in Z
a.s.

(c) Strong convergence with monotonicity and demiregularity or strong monotonicity:

If
f
is monotone and
\nabla f
is demiregular on
Z
(i.e.,
x_{n}\rightharpoonup z\in Z
and
\nabla f(x_{n})\to 0
imply
x_{n}\to z
), then
g_{n}(G_{n})\to 0
a.s. (part (a)) and
G_{n}\rightharpoonup G^{\star}
(part (b)) imply
G_{n}\to G^{\star}
strongly a.s. by
A.4
. If
f
is relatively strongly monotone with modulus
\mu>0
, i.e.,
\langle\nabla f(G)-\nabla f(G^{\prime}),G-G^{\prime}\rangle\geq\mu\|G-G^{\prime}\|_{X}^{2}
, then for
z\in Z
:

\langle\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1}),G_{n}-z\rangle\geq\langle\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1})-\nabla f(z),G_{n}-z\rangle\geq\mu\|G_{n}-z\|_{X}^{2}.

For standard Mirror-Prox:

E[D_{\phi}(z,G_{n+1})\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}]\leq D_{\phi}(z,G_{n})-\eta_{n}\mu\|G_{n}-z\|_{X}^{2}+\varepsilon_{n}(\cdot,z).

Taking expectations:

ED_{\phi}(z,G_{n+1})\leq ED_{\phi}(z,G_{n})-\eta_{n}\mu E\|G_{n}-z\|_{X}^{2}+E\varepsilon_{n}(\cdot,z).

Since
\eta_{n}\asymp 1/n
,
\sum_{n}\eta_{n}=\infty
,
\sum_{n}\eta_{n}^{2}<\infty
, and
\sum_{n}E\varepsilon_{n}(\cdot,z)<\infty
, Proposition
A.2
gives
ED_{\phi}(z,G_{n})=O(1/n)
, and
3.1
(part v.e) ensures
G_{n}\to G^{\star}
strongly a.s. For over-relaxed variants, the descent term is amplified by
\lambda_{n}(2-\lambda_{n})
, and
3.3
(part i.d) ensures strong convergence a.s. with rate
O(1/n)
, potentially with improved constants.

Convergence under random
z\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{X}_{0},P;Z)
.

Assume
E\|g_{n}(G_{n})\|_{*}^{2}\to 0
,
\sum_{n}\eta_{n}\sqrt{E\|g_{n}(G_{n})\|_{*}^{2}}<\infty
, and
\sum_{n}EY_{n}(\cdot,z)<\infty
for all
z\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{X}_{0},P;Z)
.

(a) Strong convergence of
g_{n}(G_{n})
:

For standard Mirror-Prox,
3.1
(part vi.a) gives
L^{2}
-boundedness of
(G_{n})
, and:

ED_{\phi}(z,G_{n+1})\leq ED_{\phi}(z,G_{n})-\eta_{n}E[\langle\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1}),G_{n}-z\rangle]+E\varepsilon_{n}(\cdot,z).

Since
\sum_{n}E\varepsilon_{n}(\cdot,z)<\infty
:

\sum_{n}\eta_{n}E[\langle\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1}),G_{n}-z\rangle]<\infty.

By Hölder’s inequality:

E|\langle\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1}),G_{n}-z\rangle|\leq E\|\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1})\|_{*}\|G_{n}-z\|_{X}\leq\sqrt{E\|\nabla f(\tilde{G}_{n+1})\|_{*}^{2}}\sqrt{E\|G_{n}-z\|_{X}^{2}}.

Since
(G_{n})
is
L^{2}
-bounded and
\sum_{n}\eta_{n}\sqrt{E\|g_{n}(G_{n})\|_{*}^{2}}<\infty
,
g_{n}(G_{n})\to 0
in
L^{1}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P;X^{*})
and a.s. by Lemma
A.3
.

For over-relaxed Mirror-Prox,
3.3
(part ii.a) gives:

\sum_{n}E[\lambda_{n}(2-\lambda_{n})\Theta_{n}]<\infty,

implying
\sum_{n}E[\eta_{n}^{2}\|g_{n}(\tilde{G}_{n+1})\|_{*}^{2}]<\infty
a.s., and thus
g_{n}(G_{n})\to 0
in
L^{1}
and a.s.

(b) Weak
L^{2}
convergence:

Since
\sum_{n}EY_{n}(\cdot,z)<\infty
,
3.1
(part vi.b) and
3.3
(part ii.c) ensure
(G_{n})
is
L^{2}
-bounded, and if
\mathfrak{W}(G_{n})\subset Z
a.s., then
G_{n}\rightharpoonup G^{\star}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P;Z)
in
L^{2}
and a.s.

(c) Strong
L^{1}
convergence with monotonicity and demiregularity or strong monotonicity:

If
f
is monotone and
\nabla f
is demiregular,
g_{n}(G_{n})\to 0
in
L^{1}
and a.s. (part (a)) and
G_{n}\rightharpoonup G^{\star}
(part (b)) imply
G_{n}\to G^{\star}
in
L^{1}
and a.s. by
A.4
. If
f
is relatively strongly monotone, the rate analysis from part (i.c) extends to random
z
, giving
ED_{\phi}(z,G_{n})=O(1/n)
and strong convergence in
L^{1}
and a.s. (
3.1
, part vi.e;
3.3
, part ii.d).

Geometric rates under contraction.

If
f
is relatively strongly monotone and the contraction condition of
3.2
(part iv.c) holds:

E[d_{Z,\phi}(G_{n+1})\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}]\leq\chi d_{Z,\phi}(G_{n})+c\psi_{n}\quad\text{a.s.},\quad\chi\in(0,1),

where
\psi_{n}=Y_{n}(\cdot,0)
, then:

Ed_{Z,\phi}(G_{n+1})\leq\chi^{n+1}Ed_{Z,\phi}(G_{0})+c\sum_{j=0}^{n}\chi^{n-j}E\psi_{j}.

Since
\sum_{n}E\psi_{n}<\infty
,
3.2
(part iv.c) ensures
G_{n}\to G^{\star}
strongly in
L^{2}
and a.s., with:

E\|G_{n}-G^{\star}\|_{X}^{2}\leq 4\chi^{n}Ed_{Z,\phi}^{2}(G_{0})+4c\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\chi^{n-j-1}E\psi_{j}+2c\sum_{j\geq n}E\psi_{j},

yielding a geometric rate
ED_{\phi}(G^{\star},G_{n})=O(\chi^{n})
for all variants, with over-relaxed variants improving the rate via
\lambda_{n}(2-\lambda_{n})
.

Equivalence to TRPO/PPO.

The KL-regularized objective
f(\pi)=J(\pi)+\alpha D_{\phi}(\pi,\pi_{\text{ref}})
mirrors the KL-constrained perspective of TRPO/PPO (
B.9
). The Mirror-Prox update approximates:

\pi_{n+1}\approx\arg\min_{\pi}\left\{D_{\phi}(\pi,\pi_{n})+\eta_{n}\langle\nabla J(\pi_{n})+\alpha\nabla_{\pi}D_{\phi}(\pi_{n},\pi_{\text{ref}}),\pi-\pi_{n}\rangle\right\},

equivalent to the constrained problem:

\pi_{n+1}=\arg\min_{\pi}\left\{D_{\phi}(\pi,\pi_{n})+\eta_{n}\langle\nabla f(\pi_{n}),\pi-\pi_{n}\rangle\mid D_{\phi}(\pi,\pi_{n})\leq\delta\right\}.

For over-relaxed variants, the descent term is scaled by
\lambda_{n}
(Type A) or applied via a Krasnosel’skiĭ–Mann step (Type B), aligning with
B.9
. The Bregman toolchain (
3.1
,
3.3
and
B.5
) ensures consistency, relying on established results.

∎

Appendix D
Additional Experimental Details

D.1
Adaptive Methods: AdaGrad and RMSProp

Task.
Text classification on 20 Newsgroups.

Baselines.
AdaGrad / RMSProp vs. their over-relaxed (OR) variants with
\lambda\in\{1.3,1.6,1.8\}
.

Metrics.
Training cross-entropy (CE), validation accuracy, Bregman distance
D_{\phi}
, and training loss variance.

Results.

Figure
3
summarizes both optimizers.
For AdaGrad, OR variants (especially
\lambda=1.6,1.8
) yield faster early descent in CE (a),
higher validation accuracy (b), and substantially reduced training variance (c).
The Bregman proxy (d) decreases more quickly, confirming stronger contraction.
For RMSProp, similar improvements are observed: faster CE decrease (e),
higher validation accuracy (f), and reduced variance (g),
with
\lambda=1.6
giving the best trade-off between speed and stability.
In both cases, OR consistently accelerates convergence and stabilizes updates,
validating the guarantees of
4.4
.

((a))
AdaGrad – Train CE

((b))
AdaGrad – Val Acc

((c))
AdaGrad – Variance

((d))
AdaGrad –
D_{\phi}

((e))
RMSProp – Train CE

((f))
RMSProp – Val Acc

((g))
RMSProp – Variance

((h))
RMSProp –
D_{\phi}

Figure 3:
OR-AdaGrad (top row) and OR-RMSProp (bottom row) on 20 Newsgroups.
OR variants accelerate CE descent, improve validation accuracy, reduce variance,
and lower
D_{\phi}
faster. Curves show mean
\pm
std over seeds.

Takeaway.

Over-relaxations
(\lambda\in(1,2))
consistently improve AdaGrad and RMSProp:
faster early convergence, higher accuracy, and lower variance,
in agreement with the Bregman–Fejér theory.

D.2
Natural Gradient Descent (NatGrad)

Task.
Softmax regression on MNIST.

Baselines.
Standard NatGrad (
\lambda=1.0
) vs. over-relaxed NatGrad (OR-NatGrad,
\lambda\in\{1.3,1.6,1.8\}
).

Metrics.
Training and validation negative log-likelihood (NLL), validation accuracy, Bregman distance proxy
D_{\phi}
, and natural gradient norm
\|F^{-1}g\|
.

Results.
OR-NatGrad consistently outperforms the baseline. Training and validation NLL decrease faster, validation accuracy improves by
5
–
10
percentage points, and
\|F^{-1}g\|
decays more rapidly. Among tested settings,
\lambda=1.6
achieves the best trade-off between convergence speed and stability. These outcomes confirm the theoretical guarantees of over-relaxation in NatGrad.

Figure 4:
NatGrad vs. OR-NatGrad on MNIST. Top: training and validation NLL.
Bottom left: validation accuracy. Bottom right: natural gradient norm
\|F^{-1}g\|
.
Curves show mean
\pm
std over 5 seeds. OR-NatGrad accelerates convergence and improves generalization.

D.3
Mirror-Prox and Variational Inequalities

Task.
Synthetic bilinear saddle-point problem
\min_{x}\max_{y}~x^{\top}Ay+\tfrac{\mu}{2}\|x\|^{2}-\tfrac{\mu}{2}\|y\|^{2}
.

Baselines.
Standard Mirror-Prox (
\lambda=1.0
) vs. over-relaxed Mirror-Prox (OR-MP,
\lambda\in\{1.3,1.6,1.8\}
).

Metrics.
Primal–dual gap and squared step norm
\|z_{n+1}-z_{n}\|^{2}
.

Results.
OR-MP significantly accelerates convergence: the primal–dual gap decreases faster and
\|z_{n+1}-z_{n}\|^{2}
decays more rapidly. Among tested settings,
\lambda=1.6
achieves the best trade-off between speed and stability, confirming the theoretical advantage of over-relaxation for variational inequality problems.

Figure 5:
MP vs. OR-MP on a bilinear saddle-point problem.
Left: primal–dual gap. Right: squared step norm
\|z_{k+1}-z_{k}\|^{2}
.
Curves show mean
\pm
std over 5 seeds. OR-MP accelerates convergence and improves stability.

D.4
Relative Smoothness and Strong Convexity

Task.
Multi-class logistic regression with
\ell_{2}
regularization.

Baselines.
Standard SMD (
\lambda=1.0
) vs. over-relaxed SMD (OR-SMD,
\lambda\in\{1.3,1.6,1.8\}
).

Metrics.
Expected Bregman distance
E[D_{\phi}]
.

Results.
OR-SMD consistently accelerates convergence. In the stochastic setting (A), the decay of
E[D_{\phi}]
transitions from polynomial to near-geometric, with
\lambda=1.6
offering the best trade-off. In the full-batch setting (B), OR-SMD achieves almost geometric convergence under relative smoothness and strong convexity, cutting iterations to reach a given accuracy by about half compared to the baseline.

Figure 6:
Relative smoothness and strong convexity experiments.
Left: stochastic (A). Right: full-batch (B).
OR-SMD accelerates
E[D_{\phi}]
decay from polynomial to geometric rates.

\uparrow
is better).

reduces

more quickly, achieves a lower objective, produces

balances fast convergence and stable training,
yielding improved accuracy with reduced variance.

D.7
Reinforcement Learning

Task.
Policy gradient with entropy regularization on CartPole-v1.

Baselines.
Standard PG and Mirror-Prox (MP) vs. their over-relaxed (OR) variants with
\lambda\in\{1.3,1.6,1.8\}
.

Metrics.
Average return, return variance, and policy KL divergence.

Results.
OR variants alter the trade-off between efficiency and stability.
Moderate relaxation (
\lambda=1.3,1.6
) accelerates KL decrease but can trigger instability, leading to spikes in variance and return.
In contrast,
\lambda=1.8
achieves the best stability: average return remains close to baseline while variance is reduced and KL contracts more rapidly.
These results confirm that OR can improve sample efficiency, but stability requires sufficiently strong relaxation.

Figure 10:
Policy gradient (PG) and Mirror-Prox (MP) with OR.
Top: PG returns and variances.
Bottom: PG and MP policy KL (mean
\pm
std, 5 seeds).
OR-
\lambda=1.8
provides the best stability while maintaining efficient KL reduction.
